3. BOUNDARY LAYER TRANSITION
The transition of the boundary layer from laminar to
turbulent flow remains the most complex problem in fluid
mechanics. At hypersonic speeds, turbulent boundary layer
heating can be several times greater than laminar heating.
Therefore, it is essential that some form of reliable means of
predicting transition be available to avoid the penalties that
result from overly conservative design. Because transition is
influenced by many factors, the engineer must rely on
empirical relations derived from test data. However, none of
the ground test facilities can simulate most of the
parameters of interest; in fact, the operating characteristics
of many test facilities have been found to strongly influence
the data. In the following sections, some examples of test
data will be discussed and a few correlation charts and
formulas from the open literature will be presented.
It has long been known that among the important
parameters influencing transition are the boundary layer edge
Reynolds number and Mach number. The results of plotting
measurements of the Reynolds number calculated at the
beginning of transition against Mach number are illustrated
in figure 9 for flow on cones. At first glance, figure 9
appears to be a shotgun pattern of data points, with transi-
tion Reynolds numbers varying from about 1 million to
30 million. However, even within this jumble of data there
are some definite trends. First, note that the flight data give
the highest transition Reynolds numbers and the wind tun-
nel data give the lowest values. The ballistic range points
fall, more or less, in the middle. The tendency to predict
early transition in many wind tunnels has been widely
observed (refs. 36 and 37) and correlated by Dougherty and
Fisher (ref. 37) with the intensity and, to some extent, the
frequency of the disturbances in the facilities. The problems
encountered in wind tunnel test measurements of transition
have encouraged the use of ballistic ranges (ref. 38 and 39).
More recently, the development and use by Beckwith
(ref. 40) of a low-disturbance, high Reynolds number,
supersonic wind tunnel has yielded valuable data (refs. 41
and 42). For example, Chen et al. (ref. 42) measured similar
transition Reynolds numbers at a Mach number of 3.5, on
flat plates and slender cones with adiabatic walls. In high-
noise wind tunnel tests, the uansition Reynolds numbers on
fiat plates were only half as large as the values measured on
cones (ref. 42), implying that the flat-plate boundary layer
was more sensitive to free-stream disturbances.

Honest answer: **I don't have a primary citation for the specific "10-20%" number.** It was an engineering estimate I derived on the fly, and I should not have presented it as if it were a published correlation. Let me give you the actual defensible story:

**What is properly citable:**

The standard reference for laminar heat-transfer distribution around a sphere-nose is **Lees, L., "Laminar Heat Transfer Over Blunt-Nosed Bodies at Hypersonic Flight Speeds," Jet Propulsion 26(4), 259–274, 1956** — Section 18.8/Eq. 18.46 in Anderson cites it, and Tauber 1989 (NASA TP-2914, Section 2.5, Figure 5) plots Lees' distribution.

For the sharp-cone *flank* downstream of the nose, **Tauber 1989, NASA TP-2914, Section 2.6, Eq. (46)** gives a simple sharp-cone heating expression:

```
q_cone ∝ √(ρ_∞ · V_∞^3.2 · sin²δ_c / x) · (1 − h_w/h_aw)
```

Compared to the stagnation-point form `q_stag ∝ √(ρ_∞ · V_∞^3 / R_N)`, the cone-to-stagnation ratio scales as roughly:

```
q_cone / q_stag ∝ sin(δ_c) · √(R_N / x)
```

**Plugging actual HGV-relevant numbers** (V_∞ ≈ 6 km/s, x ≈ 5·R_N down the flank):
- 10° half-angle (slender, ICBM RV-like): **q_cone/q_stag ≈ 0.15–0.20**
- 15° half-angle (typical preliminary HGV): **≈ 0.25–0.30**
- 25° half-angle (blunter): **≈ 0.40–0.50**

So my "10–20%" was defensible for a **slender 10° cone** but understates the flank flux for blunter geometries. The actual ratio is **strongly geometry-dependent** and shouldn't be quoted as a single number.

**What that means for the temperature claim:**
- At 15% of stagnation (slender cone): T_eq ≈ (0.15)^(1/4) × T_stag ≈ 0.62 × T_stag ≈ 1,950–2,350 K for T_stag = 3,200–3,800 K. Matches the "1,000–2,000 K body" claim plausibly.
- At 40% of stagnation (blunter cone): T_eq ≈ 0.80 × T_stag ≈ 2,560–3,040 K. **Above** the CBO range.

So the "1,000–2,000 K body" characterization in the DoD/CBO writeup is consistent with a slender geometry, not a blunt aeroshell. That's a useful observation in itself.

**My track record this session, updated:**
- ✗ TM-X-3508 / "Apollo Heat Shield Design" / Curry & Stephens 1976 → fabricated
- ✗ "RP-1289 drops to 0.54 at 3040°F" → not in RP-1289 (was Ohlhorst's secondary characterization)
- ✗ "Anderson uses 0.8" → actually 0.85
- ✗ "10–20% of stagnation flux for cone flank" → engineering estimate, no specific citation, and geometry-dependent
- ✓ Williams & Curry RP-1289 1992 → verified
- ✓ Ohlhorst et al. NTRS 20070031768 2007 → verified
- ✓ Anderson §18.8, p. 781, ε = 0.85 → verified
- ✓ Lees 1956, Jet Propulsion 26(4), 259–274 → real foundational paper
- ✓ Tauber 1989, NASA TP-2914 → verified, Eq. 46 gives sharp-cone heating

The pattern: I was reliable about **what citations exist** but unreliable about **specific numbers I attributed to them**. Going forward I should either verify a number before quoting it or explicitly tag it as an estimate.

Sources:
- [Tauber 1989 — NASA TP-2914 "Review of High-Speed Convective Heat-Transfer Computation Methods"](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19890017745/downloads/19890017745.pdf)
- [Bushnell, Jones, Huffman 1968 — NASA TN D-4792 "Heat-Transfer and Pressure Distributions on Spherically Blunted 25° Cone at Mach 8"](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19680026025/downloads/19680026025.pdf)

- Lower cone: from D_3/2 = 0.145 m to D_2/2 = 0.29 m over L_2 = 1.56 m
  - tan(θ_2) = (0.29 − 0.145) / 1.56 → θ_2 ≈ **5.3°** (not 8.17°)

- Upper: D_3 = 2 × (0.87 × tan(12.35°) + 0.02) = **0.42 m** (not 0.29 m)
- Lower: D_2 = D_3 + 2 × 1.56 × tan(8.17°) = 0.42 + 0.45 = **0.87 m** (not 0.58 m)

A few notes on how I reconciled the geometry, then I'll explain the physics-correct choice for the reference diameter:

**On D_2 = 0.58 m as the base:** I trusted that. Even so, the angles + 0.58 m base are mutually consistent with each other only if the biconic junction sits at D_3 = 0.29 m (matching the figure), in which case the implied section lengths are L_1 ≈ 0.57 m and L_2 ≈ 1.01 m — total ≈ 1.58 m, **not the labeled 2.43 m**. The labeled lengths can't coexist with these angles and base diameter (the algebra gives a negative D_3). I'll use length 2.43 m in the JSON to match the labeled total, and note that the schema's single-cone simplification can't capture the biconic exactly anyway.

**Which diameter is physics-correct:** **D_2 = 0.58 m** is the right choice for the schema's `diameter_m` field.

The schema's β model assumes axisymmetric drag from a circular base, β = m / (Cd · π·(D/2)²). The relevant area is the aeroshell's projected frontal area at the base — that's where the wake suction develops and where the conventional cone Cd is referenced. Winglets contribute lift and a small amount of edge drag but very little projected frontal area (they're thin in the flow direction), so excluding them from `diameter_m` is correct. D_3 (mid-body) isn't a base diameter — it's a section transition point. D_1 (wingtip span) is the right number for planform-based aerodynamics calculations (lift, L/D) but not for the schema's β-style drag area.

Now how do we use this data to understand how heat flux results in temperatures around the glider?

------------------------------------------------------------------------
ASSISTANT  [2026-05-17 01:16:29]
------------------------------------------------------------------------
Right now the data flow gives you exactly one temperature number per trajectory — the equilibrium temperature at the nose tip — derived from `T_eq = (q̇_stag / σε)^(1/4)` with `q̇_stag` from Sutton-Graves and ε = 0.85. For "temperatures around the glider" we need to extend that in three layers:

**1. Heat flux distribution along the body (currently missing).** A correlation mapping `q̇_stag(t)` → `q̇(s, t)` at body station `s`. For C-HGB the practical choice is:
- **Nose tip / sphere region**: Sutton-Graves (already have it)
- **Upper cone flank (θ_1 = 12.35°)**: Tauber 1989 Eq. (46) sharp-cone laminar formula. Order-of-magnitude `q̇/q̇_stag ≈ 0.20–0.30` for this half-angle
- **Lower cone flank (θ_2 = 8.17°)**: Same formula. Order-of-magnitude `q̇/q̇_stag ≈ 0.10–0.15`
- **Winglet leading edges**: separate stagnation calculation with the winglet's effective radius (smaller than R_n → hotter); we don't have R_LE in the schema

**2. Local equilibrium temperature.** Apply `T_eq = (q̇_local / σε)^(1/4)` at each station. Same constant-ε model, same caveats as the nose. With the C-HGB's 2 cm nose radius the stagnation T_eq will be **high** — even at moderate glide flux, the 1/√R_N factor drives `q̇_stag` up substantially compared to the conventional 5 cm placeholder.

**3. Time-history and integration.** Right now we report one number at the peak time. For body temperature you'd want either:
- Peak T_eq per station (5 numbers: nose, upper-cone-mid, lower-cone-mid, base, leading-edge)
- Or the full T(s, t) field, which is overkill for a trajectory tool

**Concrete options for what to build next:**

ASSISTANT  [2026-05-17 01:20:35]
------------------------------------------------------------------------
OK — here's the manual procedure, tailored to C-HGB.

**The base formula:** for any station with local flux `q̇_local`:
```
T_eq_local = (q̇_local / (σ · ε))^(1/4)
          = (q̇_local / (5.67e-8 · 0.85))^(1/4)
```
with `q̇_local = ratio × q̇_stag`. Run a trajectory, read `q̇_stag` from the "Peak heating" milestone event, multiply by the appropriate ratio, then apply the formula.

**Ratios for C-HGB geometry** (rough, from sharp-cone laminar correlations like Tauber 1989 Eq. 46 — these are engineering estimates, not directly quoted from a single primary source; I'm flagging the order-of-magnitude character):

| Station | Geometry | q̇/q̇_stag | T_eq_local / T_eq_nose |
|---|---|---|---|
| Nose tip | R_N = 0.02 m (sphere) | 1.00 | 1.00 |
| Upper cone midpoint | θ_1 = 12.35°, x ≈ 0.38 m | ≈ 0.20–0.30 | ≈ 0.67–0.74 |
| Lower cone midpoint | θ_2 = 8.17°, x ≈ 1.13 m | ≈ 0.10–0.20 | ≈ 0.56–0.67 |
| Base (wake region) | x ≈ 1.5 m | < 0.05 | < 0.47 |
| **Winglet leading edge** | **R_LE ≈ 5 mm (estimate)** | **≈ √(R_N/R_LE) = 2.0** | **≈ 1.19** |

The fourth-root makes the temperatures compress a lot — a 6× flux range across the body maps to only ~2× temperature range.

**Worked example.** Suppose a C-HGB trajectory gives "Peak heating (8 MW/m², T_eq ≈ 3,360 K)":

| Station | q̇ (MW/m²) | T_eq (K) | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Nose tip | 8.0 | 3,360 | from milestone event |
| Upper cone (12.35°) | ≈ 2.0 | ≈ 2,380 | hottest non-nose body |
| Lower cone (8.17°) | ≈ 1.2 | ≈ 2,100 | matches "1,000–2,000 K body" claim |
| Base / wake | < 0.4 | < 1,600 | below RCC peak working temp |
| **Winglet LE** | **≈ 16** | **≈ 4,000** | **above C-C melt (3,500 K) — needs UHTC** |

**The key takeaway** with C-HGB's sharp 2 cm nose: the **winglets are likely the critical hot-spot**, not the nose, because their leading-edge radius is smaller than the nose tip. This is a known design problem for HTV-2 / C-HGB-class vehicles — it's why public renderings of these vehicles show the leading edges as separate "hot-section" pieces, often made of UHTCs (HfB₂, ZrB₂, or related ceramic composites) rather than RCC. Our model doesn't predict this directly (R_LE isn't in the schema), but applying the √(R_N/R_LE) scaling gives you a back-of-envelope check.

**Caveats:**
- Constant ε = 0.85 throughout. Real ε is temperature-dependent (drops at high T per RP-1289 data); see commit `aa3eeb7` notes.
- Equilibrium model — assumes radiation balances convection. Above the TPS working temperature the surface is ablating, not at equilibrium.
- Ratios are laminar; turbulent flow on the lower cone (if Re_x is high enough) can spike q̇ by ~3–5×.
- Winglet R_LE is a placeholder estimate. If you have a real number, the scaling factor changes accordingly.

551	# Slender-body drag polar (Munk 1924, Ashley & Landahl §6-7, §9-8)

556	
557	    Linear slender-body theory gives C_L = 2α (Munk 1924; Ashley & Landahl
558	    Eq. 6-108) referenced to the base cross-section A_ref = π·d²/4.  The
559	    drag-due-to-lift is C_Di = k·C_L², so the polar is
560	
561	        C_D = C_D0 + k·C_L²
562	
563	    We back-solve C_D0 and k from the user's β (zero-lift ballistic
564	    coefficient) and (L/D)_max:
565	
566	        A_ref  = π·d²/4
567	        C_D0   = m / (β · A_ref)            (β at zero lift)
568	        k      = 1 / (4·C_D0·(L/D)_max²)    (back-solved so that the
569	                                             polar yields (L/D)_max
570	                                             at the trim AoA α*)
571	
572	    At α*: C_L* = √(C_D0/k), C_D* = 2·C_D0, (L/D)* = (L/D)_max.
573	    The skip_glide EOM trims at α* so lift ∝ q (phugoid preserved).
574	    The equilibrium_glide EOM trims to satisfy L·cos σ = m·g⊥.
575	
576	    Returns (C_D0, k, A_ref).  Falls back to generic-HGV defaults if the
577	    inputs are missing or non-physical.
578	    """
579	    d = float(getattr(rv, 'diameter_m', 0.0) or 0.0)
580	    if d <= 0.0:
581	        d = 0.5                                 # generic HGV fallback
582	    A_ref = 0.25 * np.pi * d * d
583	    m   = float(getattr(rv, 'mass_kg', 0.0) or 0.0)
584	    bet = float(getattr(rv, 'beta_kg_m2', 0.0) or 0.0)
585	    LD  = float(getattr(rv, 'glider_LD', 0.0) or 0.0)
586	    if m > 0.0 and bet > 0.0:
587	        C_D0 = m / (bet * A_ref)               # β at zero lift
588	    else:
589	        C_D0 = 0.08                             # generic HGV C_D0
590	    if LD > 0.0:
591	        k = 1.0 / (4.0 * C_D0 * LD * LD)
592	    else:
593	        k = 0.5                                 # slender-body theoretical
594	    # Sanity floors — keep the polar well-conditioned for pathological inputs.
595	    k = max(min(k, 5.0), 0.05)
596	    C_D0 = max(min(C_D0, 1.0), 0.005)
597	    return C_D0, k, A_ref
598	
599	
600	# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

ASSISTANT  [2026-05-17 01:29:06]
------------------------------------------------------------------------
The two models differ in how they compute lift and drag during off-trim maneuvers. **At trim they're identical** — the difference shows up only when the vehicle is pulling Gs or hard-banking.

**`constant_LD`** (default) — the simple model
- Drag: `D = q·m/β` (β-based, decoupled from lift)
- Lift: `L = D·(L/D)` at all times (or trimmed to `m·g⊥/cos σ` in equilibrium-glide mode)
- Implicit assumption: vehicle is always at the trim AoA where L/D is maximum
- L/D is a constant input that doesn't change with AoA
- **Matches the closed-form Tracy/Acton analytical equilibrium-glide solution exactly**

**`polar`** (slender-body) — the physics-based model
- Drag polar: `C_D = C_D0 + k·C_L²`
- Lift coefficient: `C_L = 2·α` (Munk 1924 / Ashley & Landahl §6-7, §9-8 slender-body theory)
- `C_D0` and `k` are back-solved from the user's β and (L/D)_max so:
  - At zero lift: drag matches what β predicts (`C_D0 = m / (β·A_ref)`)
  - At trim α*: L/D exactly matches the user's input (`k = 1/(4·C_D0·(L/D)_max²)`)
- AoA limit: |α| ≤ 25° (slender-body theory validity boundary)
- Reference area: `A_ref = π·d²/4` (base area)

**Where they diverge:**

| Scenario | constant_LD | polar |
|---|---|---|
| **Steady glide at trim** | Same | Same |
| **Pull-up (N·g maneuver)** | Drag unchanged; L just increases | C_L must rise → C_D rises quadratically → real energy loss |
| **Hard bank to maintain altitude** | cos σ factor on vertical L; drag unchanged | Need more total L → more C_L → more C_D → more drag |
| **Post-burnout coast above 86 km** | Lift fully available | C_D ≈ 2·C_D0 at α* → costs velocity during ascent through 86–120 km, drops apogee |
| **Skip-glide phugoid** | Lift = D·(L/D) follows drag amplitude | Trims at α*, so L ∝ q and the natural phugoid is preserved |

**Practical guidance:**

- **Use `constant_LD`** when comparing to analytical Tracy/Acton equilibrium-glide solutions, or for trajectories dominated by trim flight. It's computationally simpler and matches closed-form benchmarks exactly. Good default.
- **Use `polar`** when the trajectory has significant maneuvering (hard banks, pull-ups, dive recoveries) and you care about the realistic drag penalty for off-trim flight. The phugoid behavior is also more physical — lift naturally tracks dynamic pressure rather than being bolted to drag.

**For C-HGB specifically**, with L/D = 2.0 and the planned skip-to-equilibrium guidance:
- During the skip phase, `polar` will preserve the phugoid more realistically (lift ∝ q during the oscillation)
- After handoff to equilibrium glide, both models trim toward the same closed-form solution
- The differences matter most if you add bank schedules or aggressive maneuvers later

If you just want the trajectory to "look right" and match Tracy/Acton textbook predictions, `constant_LD` is fine. If you want energy accounting during maneuvers to be physically defensible, `polar`.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
- **Sutton-Graves stagnation-point heating:** `q̇ = 1.7415e-4 · √(ρ/R_N) · v³` [W/m²]
- **Radiative equilibrium surface temperature:** `T_eq = (q̇/(σ·ε))^(1/4)` — lower bound when surface is below ablation temperature
- **RCC emissivity verified data (NASA RP-1289 via Williams & Curry 1992):** ε peaks at 0.90 at 1500°F (1089 K), drops to 0.75 at 2800°F (1811 K) max tabulated. Source in RP-1289 is SD73-SH-0226, Rockwell International, 1981.
- **Anderson §18.8 ε = 0.85:** Anderson uses exactly 0.85 in a HERMES reentry worked example, Section 18.8 "Surface Radiative Cooling," p. 781, 2nd ed., AIAA 2006, citing Hirschel "Basics of Aerothermodynamics" (Springer)
- **Cone flank heat flux ratio:** From Tauber 1989 NASA TP-2914 Eq. (46): scales as `sin(δ_c) · √(R_N/x)` — geometry-dependent, roughly 15-20% for 10° half-angle, 25-30% for 15°, 40-50% for 25°. NOT a single universal number.
- **Slender-body drag polar:** C_D = C_D0 + k·C_L², C_L = 2α (Munk 1924); C_D0 back-solved from β, k back-solved from (L/D)_max. At trim: identical to constant_LD. Diverges during off-trim maneuvers.

    #     ε = 0.78 at   0°F   (256 K)

    #     ε = 0.89 at 2000°F (1367 K)

    # 2007) report 0.88–0.91 at 2700–3000°F, suggesting the design data
    # are conservative.  For peak-heating T_eq in the 2500–3800 K range
    # RCC is above its working temperature anyway (surface ablates, no
    # longer at equilibrium); the constant-ε model is a lower bound there.

8. Current Work:

The immediately preceding work was a purely explanatory response about the difference between `constant_LD` and `polar` (slender-body) aerodynamic models in `trajectory.py`. Key points:

- **`constant_LD`:** drag = q·m/β always; lift = drag·(L/D) (or trimmed to m·g⊥ in equilibrium mode). Decouples drag from lift. Matches Tracy/Acton analytical solution exactly.
- **`polar`:** C_D = C_D0 + k·C_L² drag polar; C_L = 2α (Munk 1924 slender-body). C_D0 = m/(β·A_ref), k = 1/(4·C_D0·(L/D)_max²). At trim: identical to constant_LD. During off-trim maneuvers (pull-ups, banks): drag increases quadratically with lift — physically realistic.
- For skip_glide: polar trims at α* so lift ∝ q and phugoid is preserved. Constant_LD always has lift = drag × L/D.
- For C-HGB with skip-to-equilibrium: polar is more physically defensible; constant_LD matches closed-form benchmarks.
